The total thermalized 26Al(p,γ)27Si rate is finally calculated by adding the rates
for 26Alg.s.(p,γ)27Si and 26Alm(p,γ)27Si weighted with the Boltzmann population of the
respective levels as a function of temperature. The numerical results are given in Table
7.
Appendix 26Si(p,γ)27P
The reaction rate is dominated by a single resonance at Er =318 keV corresponding
to the first excited 3/2+ state at 1125 keV in 27P. The resonance energy had recently
been revised upwards by 64 keV based on measurements of the proton energies in a 27S
β-delayed proton γ spectroscopy experiment at Lanzhou [240]. Combining this result
with the well known 26Si mass and the known excitation energy of the first excited state
in 27P from γ-ray spectroscopy leads to a revised 27P mass, that has been adopted in
AME20 and deviates significantly from previous estimates, including estimates using iso-
baric multiplets. The new resonance energy agrees well with the Coulomb dissociation
experiment performed at RIKEN [122] (315(17) keV). However, it disagrees with the
Coulomb dissociation experiment of [121] at R3B, GSI (267(20) keV) which would be
more in line with the previous estimate. We adopt in this work the resonance strength
of ωγ =2.9 meV from [240], who derive it using their measured γ-to-proton branching
ratio, and the γ-width estimated by [207]. This resonance strength is about a factor of 2
smaller than the strength derived based on a recent (d,p) transfer reaction measurement
at the Czech Academy of Science [241]. In that experiment, ANC and proton width of
the isospin mirror are extracted from data, and combined with the same γ-to-proton
branching ratio from [240], resulting in a correspondingly larger γ-width. However, the

here adopted resonance strength is in reasonable agreement with previous Coulomb Dis-
sociation experiments that obtain ωγ = 0.7 meV [121] and ωγ = 2 meV [122]. Direct
capture starts to dominate at temperatures below 0.1 GK, where the rate is too slow to
matter in X-ray bursts. Nevertheless, we include the direct capture component obtained
from [241]. For completeness we include two higher lying resonances corresponding to the
second and third excited 5/2+ states in 27P. The resonance energies of Er = 762 keV and
1054 keV have been measured using β-delayed protons [240]. For the resonance strengths,
we adopt the average of all previous measurements provided by [240] ωγ =0.65 meV, and
an average of the Coulomb Dissociation experiments [121, 122] ωγ =1 meV, respectively.
The 26Si(p,γ)27P reaction rates corrected by the SEF are given in Table 8.
Appendix 29Si(p,γ)30P
The reaction rate is derived from 13 resonances below Er = 500 keV compiled in [242]
who updated with new data from a γ-spectroscopy experiment, as well as 73 resonances
above Er = 500 keV using the resonance properties from [207]. The calculated reaction
rates, corrected with an SEF, are given in Table 8.
Appendix 29P(p,γ)30S
The rate provided in [243] is adopted here, which is largely based on their earlier
compilation [130] that summarizes previous work and new information on 30S levels from
28Si(3He,n) and 32S(p,t) transfer reaction measurements performed at the University
of Notre Dame and RCNP at Osaka University, respectively.
[243] does include an
additional resonance at Er = 820 keV (3+ state) identified in a (3He,n) γ-ray spectroscopy
experiment at Argonne National Laboratory using the Gammasphere detector array [132]
and uses an updated Q-value. [132] confirmed spins and assignments of the important
30S states to states in the 30Si isospin mirror, the latter being used to obtain the proton
spectroscopic factors. Based on the current information on 25 states above the proton
threshold in 30S, the rate at X-ray burst temperatures is dominated by a Er = 410 keV
2+ resonance, with a few additional resonances coming into play above around 1.5 GK.
For temperatures below 0.3 GK the Er = 289 keV 3+ resonance becomes dominant. An
SEF is applied and the results are given in Table 8.
Appendix 30P(p,γ)31S
Ten resonances are considered for this reaction rate. We follow largely the compila-
tion of [110], with updates from [244], and information for additional states from shell
model calculations [60]. For the resonances at Er =196 keV and 261.9 keV we use the
resonance strengths derived from (d,nγ) transfer reaction measurements at NSCL at
Michigan State University [110]. The same experiment also provided upper limits for
the resonance strengths of the Er =226.7 keV and 411.3 keV resonances, which we adopt
as values. We note that for the important 411.3 keV resonance, shell model calculations
indicate a resonance strength about a factor of 30 above the experimental upper limit.
For the important Er =259.6 keV resonance we use the resonance strengths obtained
from β-delayed proton measurements [244]. For the other resonances at Er =116 keV,

## Page 99


124.7 keV, 471 keV, 589 keV, and 618 keV we adopt the resonance strengths from shell
model calculations [60].
While these shell model calculations had indicated that the
246 keV 9/2−resonance made a non-negligible contribution, this resonance is now negli-
gible given the dominant contribution of the Er =259.6 keV resonance that had not been
predicted by the shell model. The highest energy of these considered resonances is 618
keV, which corresponds to a T = 0.8 GK Gamow window. Therefore, for T > 0.8 GK
we use the slope of the NON-SMOKER Hauser-Feshbach reaction rate [213] normalized to
the rate at T = 0.8 GK. The SEF corrected rate is listed in Table 8.
Appendix 33Cl(p,γ)34Ar
The 33Cl(p,γ)34Ar reaction rate is calculated from the updated resonance parameters
given in [138], who performed γ-spectroscopy of 34Ar populated by a fusion evaporation
reaction at Argonne National Laboratory using the GRETINA detector array. The mea-
surement results in more precise excitation energies for 4 previously known states above
the proton threshold, as well as the discovery of a fifth resonance at a resonance energy
of 397 keV corresponding to a new state in 34Ar at 5060.8 keV. [138] determined proton
spectroscopic factors and γ-widths for these 5 resonances from mirror nucleus informa-
tion, complemented with shell model calculations. The new 397 keV resonance is found
to dominate the reaction rate above 0.2 GK. The previously recommended NON-SMOKER
Hauser-Feshbach rate [213] agrees well with the new resonant rate at 0.8 GK and is used
for temperatures above 0.8 GK to account for additional higher lying resonances. The
calculated reaction rates are given in Table 9.
Appendix 34Cl(p,γ)35Ar
34Cl has a low lying excited state at 146 keV, which is a long-lived isomer under terres-
trial conditions with a half-life of 32 min. The reaction rates on ground (34Clg.s.(p,γ)35Ar)
and isomeric (34Clm(p,γ)35Ar) states are therefore evaluated separately, and in addition,
a thermalized 34Cl(p,γ)35Ar reaction rate is calculated by adding the 34Clg.s.(p,γ)35Ar
and 34Clm(p,γ)35Ar rates weighted by their respective thermal population (Eq. (4) in
[236]). According to calculation TALYS, the contributions from the higher lying thermally
populated excited states in 34Cl are negligible.
As experimental information on the relevant states in 35Ar remains too sparse for a
reaction rate calculation, we adopt the shell model calculations from [246]. They compare
calculations with spectroscopic factors of states in the 35Cl mirror nucleus obtained from
(3He,d) transfer reaction measurements at Garching [245] and (α,p) reaction studies at
the TUNL Enge split-pole spectrometer [133], as well as information on excited states in
35Ar from 36Ar(d,t)35Ar reaction measurements at Garching [131] and find the impact
of differences on the final rate to be within the quoted rate uncertainties obtained from a
Monte Carlo approach. We calculate the 34Clg.s.(p,γ)35Ar reaction rate with the param-
eters of the resonant states listed in Table II in [246], and the 34Clm(p,γ)35Ar reaction
rate with the parameters of the resonant states listed in Table III in [246].
Note that the highest energy of the listed resonances is Er = 1.16 MeV, corresponding
to the upper end of the Gamow window at T ≃1.8 GK. Therefore, above T = 1.8 GK the
NON-SMOKER Hauser-Feshbach reaction rates [213], normalized to the calculation result

at T = 1.8 GK for the ground state rate, are used. All the numerical reaction rates are

[229] calculated transition rates and concluded that the 34Cl isomeric state is in
thermal equilibrium with the ground state for temperatures above 0.23 GK, which is in
the range of typical burst ignition temperatures. In addition, for a rate NA < συ > to
matter, it must be faster than the typical rp-process time scale τ=10 s for typical densities
ρ=105 g/cm3 and hydrogen mass fractions X = 0.7. This translates into a requirement
of NA < συ > > (Yρτ)−1 = 1.4×10−6, which requires temperatures higher than about
0.23 GK for the rate to matter in X-ray bursts (see Table 10). We therefore use in our
burst calculation the thermalized reaction rate, though a non thermal population may
be possible near ignition and should be explored in future burst calculations.
Appendix 36Cl(p,α)33S
The rate of the positive Q-value reaction 36Cl(p,α)33S is evaluated considering ex-
perimental data for its reverse reaction 33S(α,p)36Cl [145].
The data cover the 0.8-
1.5 MeV/nucleon energy range and were obtained from direct inverse kinematics mea-
surements performed at the University of Notre Dame using an activation technique.
The 36Cl(p,α)33S cross sections are obtained from the experimental 33S(α,p)36Cl cross
sections using the detailed balance theorem, taking only the ground state contribution
into account. TALYS calculations are then used to reproduce the deduced 36Cl(p,α)33S
ground state cross section by adjusting the parameters of proton and α optical model
potentials. The resulting optical model potentials are then used to compute the astro-
physical reaction rate of 36Cl(p,α)33S, and the results are given in Table 10.
Appendix 35K(p,γ)36Ca
The 35K(p,γ)36Ca reaction rate is dominated by resonant capture through the first
excited 2+ state in 36Ca up to a temperature of around 2 GK. [127] performed a
37Ca(p,d)36Ca measurement at GANIL using the CRYPTA liquid hydrogen target and
determined the proton branching of this state, corresponding to a resonance at 445 keV.
Combining the measurement with a shell model calculation for the γ-partial width re-
sults in an updated resonance strength.
The numerical results, including the stellar
enhancement factor, are given in Table 10.
Appendix 42Ti(p,γ)43V
We follow the evaluation of Hou et al. [247], who determined the reaction rate from
the 8 lowest energy resonances that can be populated by l = 0, 1, 2, 3 orbital angular
momentum proton capture. Resonance energies are determined from the known excita-
tion energies in the 43Ca mirror nucleus and an updated Q-value based on a recent mass
measurement at the HIRFL cooler storage ring in Lanzhou [273]. No Coulomb shifts
are applied, instead, a 100 keV uncertainty is assumed. Proton spectroscopic factors are
taken from experimental (p,d) stripping reaction data for 43Ca when available, otherwise
the shell model is used. γ-widths were determined from the known level lifetimes in 43Ca.
A direct capture component is included as well. A SEF is applied but only impacts the

## Page 103


rate above 4 GK. The numerical astrophysical reaction rates of 42Ti(p,γ)43V are given
in Table 10.
Appendix 55Ni(p,γ)56Cu
Excitation energies of the three lowest energy resonances were determined from in-
beam γ-ray spectroscopy using a radioactive 56Ni beam impinging on a CD2 target at the
NSCL at Michigan State University [96]. Resonance states in 56Cu were populated via the
(d,2n) reaction channel. Experimental data on excitation energies were complemented
with GXPF1A shell model calculations for γ- and proton-widths, as well as for resonance
properties of higher lying resonances. More recently, the mass of 56Cu was measured
precisely using the LEBIT Penning trap at Michigan State University leading to a revised
reaction Q-value of 582(6) keV, and consequently revised resonance energies, and, using
updated shell model calculations, revised proton and γ-widths [153].
We adopt the
recommended rate from [153], corrected with a SEF, and tabulated in Table 11.
Appendix 56Ni(p,γ)57Cu
For T ≤2 GK, the reaction rate is dominated by 4 resonances at Er = 338, 418, 1708
and 1835 keV corresponding to the well known 4 first excited states in 57Cu. Recently,
proton spectroscopic factors have been determined experimentally using angle integrated
cross sections for (d,n) and (d,p) (populating the isospin mirror) transfer reactions in
inverse kinematics performed at NSCL at Michigan State University [111]. These are
important for the two lowest lying resonances, where resonance strengths are dominated
by proton widths. We adopt the resonance parameters from [111], which combine ex-
perimental data and shell model calculations to determine γ-widths. For T > 2 GK,
the slope of the NON-SMOKER [213] Hauser-Feshbach reaction rate is used, normalized to
the rate at T = 2 GK. A SEF is applied to correct the highest temperature data points
above 5 GK. Results are given in Table 11.
Appendix 57Cu(p,γ)58Zn
We adopt direct capture S-factors and resonance properties from [112], who deter-
mined excitation energies of the 6 lowest lying resonant states in 58Zn using γ-ray spec-
troscopy using the d(57Cu,58Zn)n reaction in inverse kinematics at NSCL at Michigan
State University. Resonance energies were determined using the Q-value of 2279(50) keV
given in [112], which agrees within uncertainties with the AME20 Q-value (2270(50))
[157]. Spectroscopic factors, proton- and γ-widths are calculated using fp-shell model
calculations with the GXPF1A interaction. The calculated reaction rate, corrected with
a SEF, are tabulated in Table 11.
Appendix 65As(p,γ)66Se
[274] reports a shell model calculation of the 65As(p,γ)66Se reaction rate below T =
2 GK, which is adopted in the present evaluation. Capture on the ground state and
the thermally excited first excited state in 65As is included. This calculation follows

## Page 105


[170] but uses an updated Q-value of Q = 2.469 MeV that impacts resonance energies
and associated proton penetrability. The new Q-value was determined from new 65As
and 66Se masses that were determined from the experimentally well known masses of
the mirror nuclei 65Ge and 66Ge, respectively, and Coulomb shift calculations using a
relativistic Hartree-Fock Bogoliubov approach with axially symmetric deformation. This
approach does not take advantage of the experimentally known 65As mass [248] from a
storage ring experiment adopted in AME2020, which is 184 keV larger and has a 85 keV
uncertainty. Recently, a new measurement of the mass of 65As and a first measurement of
the mass of 66Se using the same storage ring approach [249] indicate the predicted masses
are both about 50 keV too low, while the resulting central value for the new experimental
Q-value agrees with the prediction within a few keV (though a future mass evaluation
would have to consider a weighted average of the 65As mass measurements). The shell
model rate adopted here is therefore, within uncertainties, still in agreement with the
new mass measurements. The reaction rate uncertainties are large and estimated to be
at least an order of magnitude. Above T = 2 GK, the Hauser-Feshbach (HF) reaction
rate from NON-SMOKER [213] is used. Note that the HF reaction rate is normalized to the
shell model calculation at T = 2 GK. The adopted reaction rate is given in Table 11.
